I'll be thinking about three other completely different things, and I'm barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. It's just getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble paying attention like this for as long as you can remember, including when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's been like this for as long as I can remember, really. Even back in primary school, my report cards would always say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were constantly finding old worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack because I had completely forgotten to turn them in. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you also tend to feel physically restless or fidgety, like you have trouble staying still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly fidgety. I mean, I can sit still in a chair just fine... it's just that my mind isn't staying still, if that makes sense. But physically, no, I don't really feel restless or like I need to get up and move around.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you find that it happens mainly when you're worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's tied to being worried or anxious. I mean, I'm not really an anxious person in general. It just... happens. Even if I'm completely relaxed and just trying to read a book or grade a paper, my mind just wanders off on its own. It doesn't feel like worry, it just feels like my brain is changing the channel without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself sometimes when I fall behind on my grading or just lose track of things I need to do, but... no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down. My mood is generally fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. My sleep is pretty normal, and I don't get those bursts of unusual energy or anything. I'm just pretty even-keeled, aside from the trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, do you remember having the same kind of attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, even back in primary school, my report cards usually said things like... well, that I was \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were always finding these crumpled-up worksheets just shoved in the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So, yeah, it's definitely been an issue since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I know I just mentioned the \"careless\" thing from my old report cards, but as an adult... I haven't really noticed myself making a lot of actual errors. \n\nLike, when I'm grading my students' papers or putting together lesson plans, I think the details are usually fine. It's more just that it takes me a really long time to get through the work because I keep drifting off. So, I wouldn't say I make a lot of careless mistakes nowadays, I just struggle to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have this same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life, like at home or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It definitely happens in conversations. Like, in planning meetings at school, or even at home with my wife, I'll be looking right at the person, nodding along, and it seems like I'm paying attention. But then they'll ask what I think about it, and I realize I completely missed the actual point of what they just said. My mind just kind of wanders off, even when someone is speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities, like planning and keeping your work in order?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. I'm actually pretty organized. I mean, being a teacher, I kind of have to be. I keep my lesson plans structured, and my classroom files are all in order. I know exactly what I need to do and how to set it up... it's really just sitting down and staying focused on the work that's the hard part for me, not the actual planning or organizing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because I'm having a really hard time focusing on my work. I'm a secondary school teacher, and I can sit down with a stack of essays or some new curriculum document, fully intending to just get through it and do my job. \n\nBut... within ten minutes or so, I'll realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. I'll be thinking about three other completely different things, and I'm barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. It's just getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble paying attention like this for as long as you can remember, including when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's been like this for as long as I can remember, really. Even back in primary school, my report cards would always say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were constantly finding old worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack because I had completely forgotten to turn them in. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you also tend to feel physically restless or fidgety, like you have trouble staying still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly fidgety. I mean, I can sit still in a chair just fine... it's just that my mind isn't staying still, if that makes sense. But physically, no, I don't really feel restless or like I need to get up and move around.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you find that it happens mainly when you're worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's tied to being worried or anxious. I mean, I'm not really an anxious person in general. It just... happens. Even if I'm completely relaxed and just trying to read a book or grade a paper, my mind just wanders off on its own. It doesn't feel like worry, it just feels like my brain is changing the channel without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself sometimes when I fall behind on my grading or just lose track of things I need to do, but... no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down. My mood is generally fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. My sleep is pretty normal, and I don't get those bursts of unusual energy or anything. I'm just pretty even-keeled, aside from the trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, do you remember having the same kind of attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, even back in primary school, my report cards usually said things like... well, that I was \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were always finding these crumpled-up worksheets just shoved in the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So, yeah, it's definitely been an issue since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I know I just mentioned the \"careless\" thing from my old report cards, but as an adult... I haven't really noticed myself making a lot of actual errors. \n\nLike, when I'm grading my students' papers or putting together lesson plans, I think the details are usually fine. It's more just that it takes me a really long time to get through the work because I keep drifting off. So, I wouldn't say I make a lot of careless mistakes nowadays, I just struggle to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have this same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life, like at home or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It definitely happens in conversations. Like, in planning meetings at school, or even at home with my wife, I'll be looking right at the person, nodding along, and it seems like I'm paying attention. But then they'll ask what I think about it, and I realize I completely missed the actual point of what they just said. My mind just kind of wanders off, even when someone is speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities, like planning and keeping your work in order?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. At work, the missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. So, yeah, it's definitely causing real problems in both places.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because I'm having a really hard time focusing on my work. I'm a secondary school teacher, and I can sit down with a stack of essays or some new curriculum document, fully intending to just get through it and do my job. \n\nBut... within ten minutes or so, I'll realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page. I'll be thinking about three other completely different things, and I'm barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. It's just getting really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble paying attention like this for as long as you can remember, including when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's been like this for as long as I can remember, really. Even back in primary school, my report cards would always say things like, uh, \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were constantly finding old worksheets just crumpled up at the bottom of my backpack because I had completely forgotten to turn them in. So, yeah, it's definitely not a new problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you also tend to feel physically restless or fidgety, like you have trouble staying still?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I'm particularly fidgety. I mean, I can sit still in a chair just fine... it's just that my mind isn't staying still, if that makes sense. But physically, no, I don't really feel restless or like I need to get up and move around.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you find that it happens mainly when you're worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's tied to being worried or anxious. I mean, I'm not really an anxious person in general. It just... happens. Even if I'm completely relaxed and just trying to read a book or grade a paper, my mind just wanders off on its own. It doesn't feel like worry, it just feels like my brain is changing the channel without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself sometimes when I fall behind on my grading or just lose track of things I need to do, but... no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down. My mood is generally fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. My sleep is pretty normal, and I don't get those bursts of unusual energy or anything. I'm just pretty even-keeled, aside from the trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, do you remember having the same kind of attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, even back in primary school, my report cards usually said things like... well, that I was \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were always finding these crumpled-up worksheets just shoved in the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So, yeah, it's definitely been an issue since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I know I just mentioned the \"careless\" thing from my old report cards, but as an adult... I haven't really noticed myself making a lot of actual errors. \n\nLike, when I'm grading my students' papers or putting together lesson plans, I think the details are usually fine. It's more just that it takes me a really long time to get through the work because I keep drifting off. So, I wouldn't say I make a lot of careless mistakes nowadays, I just struggle to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have this same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life, like at home or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It definitely happens in conversations. Like, in planning meetings at school, or even at home with my wife, I'll be looking right at the person, nodding along, and it seems like I'm paying attention. But then they'll ask what I think about it, and I realize I completely missed the actual point of what they just said. My mind just kind of wanders off, even when someone is speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities, like planning and keeping your work in order?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. When my mind wanders, it's just... wandering. I don't get any chest pain or feel like my heart is racing or anything physical like that. It's just a focus thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't think I'm particularly fidgety. I mean, I can sit still in a chair just fine... it's just that my mind isn't staying still, if that makes sense. But physically, no, I don't really feel restless or like I need to get up and move around.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you find that it happens mainly when you're worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's tied to being worried or anxious. I mean, I'm not really an anxious person in general. It just... happens. Even if I'm completely relaxed and just trying to read a book or grade a paper, my mind just wanders off on its own. It doesn't feel like worry, it just feels like my brain is changing the channel without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself sometimes when I fall behind on my grading or just lose track of things I need to do, but... no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down. My mood is generally fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. My sleep is pretty normal, and I don't get those bursts of unusual energy or anything. I'm just pretty even-keeled, aside from the trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, do you remember having the same kind of attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, even back in primary school, my report cards usually said things like... well, that I was \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were always finding these crumpled-up worksheets just shoved in the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So, yeah, it's definitely been an issue since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I know I just mentioned the \"careless\" thing from my old report cards, but as an adult... I haven't really noticed myself making a lot of actual errors. \n\nLike, when I'm grading my students' papers or putting together lesson plans, I think the details are usually fine. It's more just that it takes me a really long time to get through the work because I keep drifting off. So, I wouldn't say I make a lot of careless mistakes nowadays, I just struggle to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have this same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life, like at home or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It definitely happens in conversations. Like, in planning meetings at school, or even at home with my wife, I'll be looking right at the person, nodding along, and it seems like I'm paying attention. But then they'll ask what I think about it, and I realize I completely missed the actual point of what they just said. My mind just kind of wanders off, even when someone is speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities, like planning and keeping your work in order?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. I'm actually pretty organized. I mean, being a teacher, I kind of have to be. I keep my lesson plans structured, and my classroom files are all in order. I know exactly what I need to do and how to set it up... it's really just sitting down and staying focused on the work that's the hard part for me, not the actual planning or organizing.\n\nDoctor: Has this focus problem caused real difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, the missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. And at home... well, my wife has pretty much started taking over paying the bills and handling all our daily reminders. She just feels like she can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. So, yeah, it's definitely causing real problems in both places.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I mean, I might drift off and stop listening, like I mentioned before, but I don't usually jump in and cut people off while they're still talking.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you also notice any physical symptoms like chest tightness, shortness of breath, stomach upset, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. When my mind wanders, it's just... wandering. I don't get any chest pain or feel like my heart is racing or anything physical like that. It's just a focus thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a little bit ago, it's definitely happening in both places. It affects me at the school with my teaching duties, and then at home with my wife having to take over the bills and everything. So, yeah, it's not just limited to one setting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I mean, I get a little frustrated with myself sometimes when I fall behind on my grading or just lose track of things I need to do, but... no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down. My mood is generally fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. My sleep is pretty normal, and I don't get those bursts of unusual energy or anything. I'm just pretty even-keeled, aside from the trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, do you remember having the same kind of attention problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um, even back in primary school, my report cards usually said things like... well, that I was \"bright but careless,\" or that I didn't finish my work unless I was closely supervised. And my parents were always finding these crumpled-up worksheets just shoved in the bottom of my backpack that I had completely forgotten to turn in. So, yeah, it's definitely been an issue since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure. I know I just mentioned the \"careless\" thing from my old report cards, but as an adult... I haven't really noticed myself making a lot of actual errors. \n\nLike, when I'm grading my students' papers or putting together lesson plans, I think the details are usually fine. It's more just that it takes me a really long time to get through the work because I keep drifting off. So, I wouldn't say I make a lot of careless mistakes nowadays, I just struggle to stay on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have this same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life, like at home or in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It definitely happens in conversations. Like, in planning meetings at school, or even at home with my wife, I'll be looking right at the person, nodding along, and it seems like I'm paying attention. But then they'll ask what I think about it, and I realize I completely missed the actual point of what they just said. My mind just kind of wanders off, even when someone is speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities, like planning and keeping your work in order?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. I'm actually pretty organized. I mean, being a teacher, I kind of have to be. I keep my lesson plans structured, and my classroom files are all in order. I know exactly what I need to do and how to set it up... it's really just sitting down and staying focused on the work that's the hard part for me, not the actual planning or organizing.\n\nDoctor: Has this focus problem caused real difficulties in more than one area of your life, such as at work, at home, or in relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. At work, the missed follow-through has actually gotten serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. And at home... well, my wife has pretty much started taking over paying the bills and handling all our daily reminders. She just feels like she can’t count on me to keep track of them anymore. So, yeah, it's definitely causing real problems in both places.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I do that. I mean, I might drift off and stop listening, like I mentioned before, but I don't usually jump in and cut people off while they're still talking.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you also notice any physical symptoms like chest tightness, shortness of breath, stomach upset, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. When my mind wanders, it's just... wandering. I don't get any chest pain or feel like my heart is racing or anything physical like that. It's just a focus thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a little bit ago, it's definitely happening in both places. It affects me at the school with my teaching duties, and then at home with my wife having to take over the bills and everything. So, yeah, it's not just limited to one setting.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders, do you ever lose time or find that you can't remember what happened for a stretch of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't lose time or black out or anything like that. It's more like... I just realize I haven't been paying attention to what's right in front of me. I still know where I am and what time it is, I just wasn't focused on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge even when there isn't an obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't feel nervous or on edge like that. I might get a little stressed if I'm behind on grading papers or something, but it's always because of a specific reason. I don't just feel anxious out of nowhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It's just a focus thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention problems in more than one setting, like both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a little bit ago, it's definitely happening in both places. It affects me at the school with my teaching duties, and then at home with my wife having to take over the bills and everything. I still know where I am and what time it is, I just wasn't focused on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge even when there isn't an obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It affects me at the school with my teaching duties, and then at home with my wife having to take over the bills and everything. So, yeah, it's not just limited to one setting.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders, do you ever lose time or find that you can't remember what happened for a stretch of time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't lose time or black out or anything like that. It's more like... I just realize I haven't been paying attention to what's right in front of me. I still know where I am and what time it is, I just wasn't focused on what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge even when there isn't an obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't feel nervous or on edge like that. I might get a little stressed if I'm behind on grading papers or something, but it's always because of a specific reason. I don't just feel anxious out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss important details when you're doing tasks?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I make careless mistakes or miss details, really. I mean, I might lose focus or take longer to get through things, but when I actually do the work, like grading or lesson planning, I usually get the details right. It's more about the struggle to stay on task rather than doing the task incorrectly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel detached from yourself, like you're watching yourself from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. I don't feel detached or like I'm watching myself. I mean, I might zone out and get lost in my thoughts, but I'm always still right here in my own body."
